					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">4th-century Christian saint</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the fourth-century Christian saint. For the gift-bearing figure in modern folklore and popular culture, see <a href="/wiki/Santa_Claus" title="Santa Claus">Santa Claus</a>. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Saint_Nicholas_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Saint Nicholas (disambiguation)">Saint  Nicholas (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Nicholas of Myra" redirects here. Not to be confused with <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_of_Lyra" title="Nicholas of Lyra">Nicholas of Lyra</a>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">

</p>

<p><b>Saint Nicholas of Myra</b><sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> (traditionally 15 March 270 – 6 December 343),<sup id="cite_ref-center_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-center-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> also known as <b>Nicholas of Bari</b>, was an <a href="/wiki/Early_Christian" class="mw-redirect" title="Early Christian">early Christian</a> bishop of <a href="/wiki/Greeks" title="Greeks">Greek descent</a> from the maritime city of <a href="/wiki/Myra" title="Myra">Myra</a> in <a href="/wiki/Asia_Minor" class="mw-redirect" title="Asia Minor">Asia Minor</a> (<a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Μύρα</span></span>; modern-day <a href="/wiki/Demre" title="Demre">Demre</a>, Turkey) during the time of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman Empire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Because of the many miracles attributed to his <a href="/wiki/Intercession" title="Intercession">intercession</a>, he is also known as <b>Nicholas the Wonderworker</b>.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup>  Saint Nicholas is the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of sailors, merchants, archers, repentant thieves, children, brewers, pawnbrokers, unmarried people, and students in various cities and countries around Europe. His reputation evolved among the pious, as was common for <a href="/wiki/Early_Christian_saints" class="mw-redirect" title="Early Christian saints">early Christian saints</a>, and his legendary habit of secret gift-giving gave rise to the traditional model of <a href="/wiki/Santa_Claus" title="Santa Claus">Santa Claus</a> ("Saint Nick") through <a href="/wiki/Sinterklaas" title="Sinterklaas">Sinterklaas</a>.
</p><p>Little is known about the historical Saint Nicholas. The earliest accounts of his life were written centuries after his death and probably contain legendary elaborations. He is said to have been born in the Greek seaport of <a href="/wiki/Patara_(Lycia)" title="Patara (Lycia)">Patara</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lycia_et_Pamphylia" title="Lycia et Pamphylia">Lycia</a>, in Asia Minor to wealthy Christian parents.<sup id="cite_ref-Collins,_Ace_2009_121_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Collins,_Ace_2009_121-12">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>In one of the earliest attested and most famous incidents from his life, he is said to have rescued three girls from being <a href="/wiki/Forced_into_prostitution" class="mw-redirect" title="Forced into prostitution">forced into prostitution</a> by dropping a sack of gold coins through the window of their house each night for three nights so their father could pay a <a href="/wiki/Dowry" title="Dowry">dowry</a> for each of them. Other early stories tell of him calming a storm at sea, saving three innocent soldiers from wrongful execution, and chopping down a tree possessed by a demon. In his youth, he is said to have made a pilgrimage to <a href="/wiki/Roman_Egypt" title="Roman Egypt">Egypt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Syria_Palaestina" title="Syria Palaestina">Palestine</a>. Shortly after his return, he became <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Myra" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Myra">Bishop of Myra</a>. He was later cast into prison during the <a href="/wiki/Persecution_of_Diocletian" class="mw-redirect" title="Persecution of Diocletian">persecution of Diocletian</a>, but was released after the accession of <a href="/wiki/Constantine_the_Great" title="Constantine the Great">Constantine</a>. 
</p><p>An early list makes him an attendee at the <a href="/wiki/First_Council_of_Nicaea" title="First Council of Nicaea">First Council of Nicaea</a> in 325, but he is never mentioned in any writings by people who were at the council. Late, unsubstantiated legends claim that he was temporarily defrocked and imprisoned during the council for slapping the heretic <a href="/wiki/Arius" title="Arius">Arius</a>. Another famous late legend tells how he resurrected three children, who had been murdered and pickled in brine by a butcher planning to sell them as pork during a famine.
</p><p>Fewer than 200 years after Nicholas's death, the <a href="/wiki/St._Nicholas_Church,_Demre" title="St. Nicholas Church, Demre">St. Nicholas Church</a> was built in Myra under the orders of <a href="/wiki/Theodosius_II" title="Theodosius II">Theodosius II</a> over the site of the church where he had served as bishop, and his remains were moved to a sarcophagus in that church. 
</p><p>In 1087, while the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Greeks" title="Byzantine Greeks">Greek Christian inhabitants</a> of the region were subjugated by the newly arrived Muslim <a href="/wiki/Seljuk_Turks" class="mw-redirect" title="Seljuk Turks">Seljuk Turks</a>, and soon after their church was declared to be in <a href="/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism" title="East–West Schism">schism</a> by the Catholic church, a group of merchants from the Italian city of <a href="/wiki/Bari" title="Bari">Bari</a> removed the major bones of Nicholas's skeleton from his sarcophagus in the church without authorization and brought them to their hometown, where they are now enshrined in the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_di_San_Nicola" title="Basilica di San Nicola">Basilica di San Nicola</a>. The remaining bone fragments from the sarcophagus were later removed by Venetian sailors and taken to <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Venice" title="Republic of Venice">Venice</a> during the <a href="/wiki/First_Crusade" title="First Crusade">First Crusade</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Biographical_sources">Biographical sources</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Biographical sources">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Very little at all is known about Saint Nicholas's historical life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010vii–x_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010vii–x-13">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal200514–15_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal200514–15-14">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Any writings Nicholas himself may have produced have been lost and he is not mentioned by any contemporary chroniclers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal200514_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal200514-15">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> This is not surprising,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010vii–viii_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010vii–viii-16">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> since Nicholas lived during <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Roman_Empire#284–301:_Diocletian_and_the_Tetrarchy" title="History of the Roman Empire">a turbulent time in Roman history</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010vii–viii_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010vii–viii-16">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Furthermore, all written records were kept on <a href="/wiki/Papyrus" title="Papyrus">papyrus</a> or <a href="/wiki/Parchment" title="Parchment">parchment</a>, which were less durable than modern paper, and texts needed to be periodically recopied by hand onto new material in order to be preserved.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010viii_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010viii-17">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> The earliest mentions of Saint Nicholas indicate that, by the sixth century, his <a href="/wiki/Cult_(religious_practice)" title="Cult (religious practice)">cult</a> was already well-established.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013250_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013250-18">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Less than two hundred years after Saint Nicholas's probable death, the Eastern Roman Emperor <a href="/wiki/Theodosius_II" title="Theodosius II">Theodosius II</a> (ruled 401–450) ordered the building of the <a href="/wiki/St._Nicholas_Church,_Demre" title="St. Nicholas Church, Demre">Church of Saint Nicholas in Myra</a>, which thereby preserves an early mention of his name.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010ix_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010ix-19">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> The Byzantine historian <a href="/wiki/Procopius" title="Procopius">Procopius</a> also mentions that the Emperor <a href="/wiki/Justinian_I" title="Justinian I">Justinian I</a> (ruled 527–565) renovated churches in <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a> dedicated to Saint Nicholas and <a href="/wiki/Priscus_(saint)" title="Priscus (saint)">Saint Priscus</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013251_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013251-20">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010ix_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010ix-19">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> which may have originally been built as early as <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 490.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013251_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013251-20">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nicholas's name also occurs as "Nicholas of Myra of Lycia" on the tenth line of a list of attendees at the Council of Nicaea recorded by the historian <a href="/wiki/Theodoret" title="Theodoret">Theodoret</a> in the <i><a href="/wiki/Historiae_Ecclesiasticae_Tripartitae_Epitome" title="Historiae Ecclesiasticae Tripartitae Epitome">Historiae Ecclesiasticae Tripartitae Epitome</a></i>, written sometime between 510 and 515.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010ix_19-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010ix-19">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013250_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013250-18">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> A single, offhand mention of Nicholas of Myra also occurs in the biography of another saint, Saint <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_of_Sion" title="Nicholas of Sion">Nicholas of Sion</a>, who apparently took the name "Nicholas" to honor him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal200514–15_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal200514–15-14">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010x_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010x-21">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> <i>The Life of Saint Nicholas of Sion</i>, written around 250 years after Nicholas of Myra's death, briefly mentions Nicholas of Sion visiting Nicholas's tomb to pay homage to him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal200514–15_14-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal200514–15-14">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010x_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010x-21">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013250_18-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013250-18">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> According to Jeremy Seal, the fact that Nicholas had a tomb that could be visited serves as the almost solitary definitive proof that he was a real historical figure.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal200515_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal200515-22">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010x_21-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010x-21">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his treatise <i>De statu animarum post mortem</i> (written <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 583), the theologian <a href="/wiki/Eustratius_of_Constantinople" class="mw-redirect" title="Eustratius of Constantinople">Eustratius of Constantinople</a> cites Saint Nicholas of Myra's miracle of the three counts as evidence that souls may work independent from the body.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013251_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013251-20">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Eustratius credits a <a href="/wiki/Lost_work" class="mw-redirect" title="Lost work">lost</a> <i>Life of Saint Nicholas</i> as his source. Nearly all the sources Eustratius references date from the late fourth century to early fifth century, indicating the <i>Life of Saint Nicholas</i> to which he refers was probably written during this time period, shortly after Nicholas's death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013251_20-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013251-20">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> The earliest complete account of Nicholas's life that has survived to the present is a <i>Life of Saint Nicholas</i>, written in the early ninth century by <a href="/w/index.php?title=Michael_the_Archimandrite&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Michael the Archimandrite (page does not exist)">Michael the Archimandrite</a> (814–842), nearly 500 years after Nicholas's probable death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010xi_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010xi-23">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite its extremely late date, Michael the Archimandrite's <i>Life of Saint Nicholas</i> is believed to heavily rely on older written sources and oral traditions.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> The identity and reliability of these sources, however, remains uncertain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Catholic historian D. L. Cann and <a href="/wiki/Medieval_studies" title="Medieval studies">medievalist</a> <a href="/wiki/Charles_W._Jones_(medievalist)" title="Charles W. Jones (medievalist)">Charles W. Jones</a> both consider Michael the Archimandrite's <i>Life</i> the only account of Saint Nicholas that is likely to contain any historical truth.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010xi_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010xi-23">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Jona_Lendering" title="Jona Lendering">Jona Lendering</a>, a Dutch historian of classical antiquity, notes that Michael the Archimandrite's <i>Life</i> does not contain a "<a href="/wiki/Conversion_narrative" title="Conversion narrative">conversion narrative</a>", which was unusual for saints' lives of the period when it was written. He therefore argues that it is possible Michael the Archimandrite may have been relying on a source written before conversion narratives became popular, which would be a positive indication of that source's reliability.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> He notes that many of the stories recounted by Michael the Archimandrite closely resemble stories told about the first-century AD <a href="/wiki/Neopythagoreanism" title="Neopythagoreanism">Neopythagorean</a> philosopher <a href="/wiki/Apollonius_of_Tyana" title="Apollonius of Tyana">Apollonius of Tyana</a> in the <i><a href="/wiki/Life_of_Apollonius_of_Tyana" title="Life of Apollonius of Tyana">Life of Apollonius of Tyana</a></i>, an eight-volume biography of him written in the early third century by the Greek writer <a href="/wiki/Philostratus" title="Philostratus">Philostratus</a>. Christian storytellers were known to adapt older pagan legends and attribute them to Christian saints. As Apollonius's hometown of <a href="/wiki/Tyana" title="Tyana">Tyana</a> was not far from Myra, Lendering contends that many popular stories about Apollonius may have become attached to Saint Nicholas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life_and_legends">Life and legends</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Life and legends">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Family_and_background">Family and background</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Family and background">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Accounts of Saint Nicholas's life agree on the essence of his story, but modern historians disagree regarding how much of this story is actually rooted in historical fact.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013249_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013249-26">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Traditionally, Nicholas was born in the city of <a href="/wiki/Patara,_Lycia" class="mw-redirect" title="Patara, Lycia">Patara</a> (<a href="/wiki/Lycia_et_Pamphylia" title="Lycia et Pamphylia">Lycia et Pamphylia</a>), a port on the <a href="/wiki/Mediterranean_Sea" title="Mediterranean Sea">Mediterranean Sea</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Collins,_Ace_2009_121_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Collins,_Ace_2009_121-12">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> in <a href="/wiki/Asia_Minor" class="mw-redirect" title="Asia Minor">Asia Minor</a> in the Roman Empire, to a wealthy family of <a href="/wiki/Greeks_in_Turkey#History" title="Greeks in Turkey">Greek</a> Christians.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013249_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013249-26">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Ingram,_W._Scott;_Ingram,_Asher_2004_24_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ingram,_W._Scott;_Ingram,_Asher_2004_24-29">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Collins,_Ace_2009_121_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Collins,_Ace_2009_121-12">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> According to some accounts, his parents were named Epiphanius (<span title="Greek-language text"><span lang="el">Ἐπιφάνιος</span></span>, <span title="Greek-language romanization"><i lang="el-Latn"><i>Epiphánios</i></i></span>) and Johanna (<span title="Greek-language text"><span lang="el">Ἰωάννα</span></span>, <span title="Greek-language romanization"><i lang="el-Latn"><i>Iōánna</i></i></span>),<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> but, according to others, they were named Theophanes (<span title="Greek-language text"><span lang="el">Θεοφάνης</span></span>, <span title="Greek-language romanization"><i lang="el-Latn"><i>Theophánēs</i></i></span>) and Nonna (<span title="Greek-language text"><span lang="el">Νόννα</span></span>, <span title="Greek-language romanization"><i lang="el-Latn"><i>Nónna</i></i></span>).<sup id="cite_ref-Collins,_Ace_2009_121_12-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Collins,_Ace_2009_121-12">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> In some accounts, Nicholas's uncle was the bishop of the city of <a href="/wiki/Myra" title="Myra">Myra</a>, also in Lycia. Recognizing his nephew's calling, Nicholas's uncle ordained him as a priest.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Generosity_and_travels">Generosity and travels</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Generosity and travels">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Gentile_da_Fabriano_063.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Gentile_da_Fabriano_063.jpg/220px-Gentile_da_Fabriano_063.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="221" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Gentile_da_Fabriano_063.jpg/330px-Gentile_da_Fabriano_063.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Gentile_da_Fabriano_063.jpg/440px-Gentile_da_Fabriano_063.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2024" data-file-height="2035" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Gentile_da_Fabriano_063.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The dowry for the three virgins (Gentile da Fabriano, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1425, <a href="/wiki/Pinacoteca_Vaticana" class="mw-redirect" title="Pinacoteca Vaticana">Pinacoteca Vaticana</a>, Rome)</div></div></div>
<p>After his parents died, Nicholas is said to have distributed their wealth to the poor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> In his most famous exploit,<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> which is first attested in Michael the Archimandrite's <i>Life of Saint Nicholas</i>, Nicholas heard of a devout man who had once been wealthy but had lost all of his money due to the "plotting and envy of <a href="/wiki/Satan" title="Satan">Satan</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Michael10–11_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Michael10–11-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> The man could not afford proper <a href="/wiki/Dowry" title="Dowry">dowries</a> for his three daughters.<sup id="cite_ref-Michael10–11_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Michael10–11-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> This meant that they would remain unmarried and probably, in absence of any other possible employment, be forced to become prostitutes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Michael10–11_34-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Michael10–11-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Hearing of the girls' plight, Nicholas decided to help them, but, being too modest to help the family in public (or to save them the humiliation of accepting charity), he went to the house under the cover of night and threw a <a href="/wiki/Money_purse" class="mw-redirect" title="Money purse">purse</a> filled with gold coins through the window opening into the house.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> The father immediately arranged a marriage for his first daughter, and after her wedding, Nicholas threw a second bag of gold through the same window late at night.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Michael12–18_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Michael12–18-37">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Michael the Archimandrite's account, after the second daughter was married, the father stayed awake for at least two "nights" and caught Saint Nicholas in the same act of charity toward the third daughter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Michael16–18_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Michael16–18-38">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The father fell on his knees, thanking him, and Nicholas ordered him not to tell anyone about the gifts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Michael16–18_38-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Michael16–18-38">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The scene of Nicholas's secret gift-giving is one of the most popular scenes in Christian devotional art, appearing in icons and frescoes from across Europe. Although depictions vary depending on time and place,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal20051_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal20051-39">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Nicholas is often shown wearing a <a href="/wiki/Cowl" title="Cowl">cowl</a> while the daughters are typically shown in bed, dressed in their nightclothes. Many renderings contain a <a href="/wiki/Cypress" title="Cypress">cypress</a> tree or a cross-shaped <a href="/wiki/Cupola" title="Cupola">cupola</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal20051_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal20051-39">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The historicity of this incident is disputed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Adam C. English argues for a historical kernel to the legend, noting the story's early attestation as well as the fact that no similar stories were told about any other Christian saints.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglishCrumm2012_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglishCrumm2012-40">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Jona Lendering, who also argues for the story's authenticity, notes that a similar story is told in Philostratus's <i>Life of Apollonius of Tyana</i>, in which Apollonius gives money to an impoverished father but posits that Michael the Archimandrite's account is markedly different.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Philostratus does not mention the fate of the daughters and, in his story, Apollonius's generosity is purely motivated out of sympathy for the father; in Michael the Archimandrite's account, however, Saint Nicholas is instead expressly stated to be motivated by a desire to save the daughters from being sold into prostitution.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> He argues that this desire to help women is most characteristic of fourth-century Christianity, due to the prominent role women played in the early Christian movement, rather than Greco-Roman paganism or the Christianity of Michael the Archimandrite's time in the ninth century, by which point the position of women had drastically declined.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In another story, Nicholas is said to have visited the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Land" title="Holy Land">Holy Land</a>. The ship he was on was nearly destroyed by a terrible storm but he rebuked the waves, causing the storm to subside. Thus, Nicholas became venerated as the patron saint of sailors and travelers. <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Bishop_of_Myra">Bishop of Myra</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Bishop of Myra">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:262px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ilja_Jefimowitsch_Repin_-_Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_saves_three_innocents_from_death.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Ilja_Jefimowitsch_Repin_-_Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_saves_three_innocents_from_death.jpg/260px-Ilja_Jefimowitsch_Repin_-_Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_saves_three_innocents_from_death.jpg" decoding="async" width="260" height="273" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Ilja_Jefimowitsch_Repin_-_Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_saves_three_innocents_from_death.jpg/390px-Ilja_Jefimowitsch_Repin_-_Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_saves_three_innocents_from_death.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Ilja_Jefimowitsch_Repin_-_Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_saves_three_innocents_from_death.jpg/520px-Ilja_Jefimowitsch_Repin_-_Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_saves_three_innocents_from_death.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1524" data-file-height="1600" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ilja_Jefimowitsch_Repin_-_Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_saves_three_innocents_from_death.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>Saint Nicholas Saves Three Innocents from Death</i> (1888) by <a href="/wiki/Ilya_Repin" title="Ilya Repin">Ilya Repin</a></div></div></div>
<p>After visiting the Holy Land, Nicholas returned to Myra. The bishop of Myra, who had succeeded Nicholas's uncle, had recently died<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> and the priests in the city had decided that the first priest to enter the church that morning would be made bishop. Nicholas went to the church to pray<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> and was therefore proclaimed the new bishop.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013249_26-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013249-26">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> He is said to have been <a href="/wiki/Confessor_of_the_Faith" title="Confessor of the Faith">imprisoned and tortured</a> during the <a href="/wiki/Great_Persecution" class="mw-redirect" title="Great Persecution">Great Persecution</a> under the Emperor <a href="/wiki/Diocletian" title="Diocletian">Diocletian</a> (ruled 284–305),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013249–250_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013249–250-42">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilkinson2018163_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilkinson2018163-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> but was released under the orders of the Emperor <a href="/wiki/Constantine_the_Great" title="Constantine the Great">Constantine the Great</a> (ruled 306–337).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013250_18-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013250-18">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> This story sounds plausible, but is not attested in the earliest sources and is therefore unlikely to be historical.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One of the earliest attested stories of Saint Nicholas is one in which he saves three innocent men from execution.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler201038_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler201038-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Michael31_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Michael31-45">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> According to Michael the Archimandrite, three innocent men were condemned to death by the governor Eustathius. As they were about to be executed, Nicholas appeared, pushed the executioner's sword to the ground, released them from their chains, and angrily chastised a juror who had accepted a bribe.<sup id="cite_ref-Michael31_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Michael31-45">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> According to Jona Lendering, this story directly parallels an earlier story in Philostratus's <i>Life of Apollonius of Tyana</i>, in which Apollonius prevents the execution of a man falsely condemned of banditry.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Michael the Archimandrite also tells another story in which the consul <a href="/wiki/Ablabius_(consul)" title="Ablabius (consul)">Ablabius</a> accepted a bribe to put three famous generals to death, in spite of their actual innocence.<sup id="cite_ref-Michael33_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Michael33-46">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Saint Nicholas appeared to Constantine and Ablabius in dreams, informing Constantine of the truth and frightening Ablabius into releasing the generals, for fear of <a href="/wiki/Hell" title="Hell">Hell</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Michael33_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Michael33-46">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Later versions of the story are more elaborate, interweaving the two stories together. According to one version, Emperor Constantine sent three of his most trusted generals, named Ursos, Nepotianos, and Herpylion, to put down a rebellion in <a href="/wiki/Phrygia" title="Phrygia">Phrygia</a>. However, a storm forced them to take refuge in Myra.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler201038_35-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler201038-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Unbeknownst to the generals, who were in the harbor, their soldiers further inland were fighting with local merchants and engaging in looting and destruction. Nicholas confronted the generals for allowing their soldiers to misbehave and the generals brought an end to the looting.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler201038–39_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler201038–39-47">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Immediately after the soldiers had returned to their ships, Nicholas heard word of the three innocent men about to be executed and the three generals aided him in stopping the execution. Eustathius attempted to flee on his horse but Nicholas stopped his horse and chastised him for his corruption.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler201039–40_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler201039–40-48">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Eustathius, under the threat of being reported directly to the Emperor, repented of his corrupt ways.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler201040_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler201040-49">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Afterward, the generals succeeded in ending the rebellion and were promoted by Constantine to even higher status.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler201040_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler201040-49">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> The generals' enemies, however, slandered them to the consul Ablabius, telling him that they had not really put down the revolt, but instead encouraged their own soldiers to join it. The generals' enemies also bribed Ablabius and he had the three generals imprisoned. Nicholas then made his dream appearances and the three generals were set free.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler201040–41_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler201040–41-50">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Council_of_Nicaea">Council of Nicaea</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Council of Nicaea">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_slapping_Arius_at_the_Council_of_Nicaea_Greek_Icon.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_slapping_Arius_at_the_Council_of_Nicaea_Greek_Icon.jpg/220px-Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_slapping_Arius_at_the_Council_of_Nicaea_Greek_Icon.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="142" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_slapping_Arius_at_the_Council_of_Nicaea_Greek_Icon.jpg/330px-Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_slapping_Arius_at_the_Council_of_Nicaea_Greek_Icon.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_slapping_Arius_at_the_Council_of_Nicaea_Greek_Icon.jpg/440px-Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_slapping_Arius_at_the_Council_of_Nicaea_Greek_Icon.jpg 2x" data-file-width="450" data-file-height="291" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Nicholas_of_Myra_slapping_Arius_at_the_Council_of_Nicaea_Greek_Icon.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Detail of a late medieval <a href="/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church" title="Greek Orthodox Church">Greek Orthodox</a> fresco showing Saint Nicholas slapping <a href="/wiki/Arius" title="Arius">Arius</a> at the <a href="/wiki/First_Council_of_Nicaea" title="First Council of Nicaea">First Council of Nicaea</a>, a famous incident whose historicity is disputed<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>In 325, Nicholas is said to have attended the <a href="/wiki/First_Council_of_Nicaea" title="First Council of Nicaea">First Council of Nicaea</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013250_18-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013250-18">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> where he is said to have been a staunch opponent of <a href="/wiki/Arianism" title="Arianism">Arianism</a> and a devoted supporter of <a href="/wiki/Trinitarianism" class="mw-redirect" title="Trinitarianism">Trinitarianism</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> and one of the bishops who signed the <a href="/wiki/Nicene_Creed" title="Nicene Creed">Nicene Creed</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Davis,_Leo_Donald;_1990_58_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Davis,_Leo_Donald;_1990_58-54">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Nicholas's attendance at the Council of Nicaea is attested early by Theodore the Lector's list of attendees, which records him as the 151st attendee.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013250_18-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackerBurgessOgden2013250-18">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010ix_19-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010ix-19">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> However, he is conspicuously never mentioned by <a href="/wiki/Athanasius_of_Alexandria" title="Athanasius of Alexandria">Athanasius of Alexandria</a>, the foremost defender of Trinitarianism at the council, who knew all the notable bishops of the period,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010xii_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler2010xii-55">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> nor is he mentioned by the historian <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a>, who was also present at the council.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal200514_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal200514-15">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Adam C. English notes that lists of the attendees at Nicaea vary considerably, with shorter lists only including roughly 200 names, but longer lists including around 300. Saint Nicholas's name only appears on the longer lists, not the shorter ones.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglishCrumm2012_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglishCrumm2012-40">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Nicholas's name appears on a total of three early lists, one of which, Theodore the Lector, is generally considered to be the most accurate. According to Jona Lendering, there are two main possibilities:
</p>
<ol><li>Nicholas <i>did not</i> attend the Council of Nicaea, but someone at an early date was baffled that his name was not listed and so added him to the list.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Many scholars tend to favor this explanation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal200593_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal200593-56">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016_51-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Nicholas <i>did</i> attend the Council of Nicaea, but, at an early date, someone decided to remove his name from the list, apparently deciding that it was better if no one remembered he had been there.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup></li></ol>
<p>A later legend, first attested in the fourteenth century, over 1,000 years after Nicholas's death, holds that, during the Council of Nicaea, Nicholas lost his temper and slapped "a certain Arian" across the face. On account of this, Constantine revoked Nicholas's <a href="/wiki/Mitre" title="Mitre">miter</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pallium" title="Pallium">pallium</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016_51-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Steven D. Greydanus<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Words_to_watch#Unsupported_attributions" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Words to watch"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too-vague attribution or weasel words. (December 2022)">who?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> concludes that, because of the story's late attestation, it "has no historical value."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016_51-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Jona Lendering, however, defends the veracity and historicity of the incident, arguing that, <a href="/wiki/Criterion_of_embarrassment" title="Criterion of embarrassment">as it was embarrassing</a> and reflects poorly on Nicholas's reputation, it is inexplicable why later hagiographers would have invented it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Later versions of the legend embellish it, making the heretic <a href="/wiki/Arius" title="Arius">Arius</a> himself<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016_51-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler201035_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler201035-57">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> and having Nicholas punch him rather than merely slapping him with his open hand. In these versions of the story, Nicholas is also imprisoned,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016_51-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler201035_57-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler201035-57">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> but Christ and the Virgin Mary appear to him in his cell.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016_51-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler201035_57-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler201035-57">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> He tells them he is imprisoned "for loving you" and they free him from his chains and restore his vestments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016_51-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler201035_57-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler201035-57">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> The scene of Nicholas slapping Arius is celebrated in Eastern Orthodox icons <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016_51-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreydanus2016-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> and episodes of Saint Nicholas at Nicaea are shown in a series of paintings from the 1660s in the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_di_San_Nicola" title="Basilica di San Nicola">Basilica di San Nicola</a> in <a href="/wiki/Bari" title="Bari">Bari</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal200593_56-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal200593-56">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_reputed_miracles">Other reputed miracles</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Other reputed miracles">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Nicolas_Heures_d%27Anne_de_Bretagne.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Saint_Nicolas_Heures_d%27Anne_de_Bretagne.jpg/220px-Saint_Nicolas_Heures_d%27Anne_de_Bretagne.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="345" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Saint_Nicolas_Heures_d%27Anne_de_Bretagne.jpg/330px-Saint_Nicolas_Heures_d%27Anne_de_Bretagne.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Saint_Nicolas_Heures_d%27Anne_de_Bretagne.jpg/440px-Saint_Nicolas_Heures_d%27Anne_de_Bretagne.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3029" data-file-height="4746" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Nicolas_Heures_d%27Anne_de_Bretagne.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Illustration of Saint Nicholas resurrecting the three butchered children from the <i><a href="/wiki/Grandes_Heures_of_Anne_of_Brittany" title="Grandes Heures of Anne of Brittany">Grandes Heures d'Anne de Bretagne</a></i> (created between 1503 and 1508)</div></div></div>
<p>One story tells how during a terrible famine, a malicious butcher lured three little children into his house, where he killed them, placing their remains in a barrel to cure, planning to sell them off as ham.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Nicholas, visiting the region to care for the hungry, saw through the butcher's lies and resurrected the pickled children by making the <a href="/wiki/Sign_of_the_cross" title="Sign of the cross">sign of the cross</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish2016132_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish2016132-59">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Jona Lendering opines that the story is "without any historical value".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Adam C. English notes that the story of the resurrection of the pickled children is a late medieval addition to the legendary biography of Saint Nicholas and that it is not found in any of his earliest <i>Lives</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglishCrumm2012_40-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglishCrumm2012-40">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Although this story seems bizarre and horrifying to modern audiences, it was tremendously popular throughout the <a href="/wiki/Late_Middle_Ages" title="Late Middle Ages">Late Middle Ages</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Early_modern_period" title="Early modern period">early modern period</a>, and widely beloved by ordinary folk.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish2016132_59-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish2016132-59">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136_32-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1976136-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint_44-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> It is depicted in stained glass windows, wood panel paintings, tapestries, and frescoes. Eventually, the scene became so widely reproduced that, rather than showing the whole scene, artists began to merely depict Saint Nicholas with three naked children and a wooden barrel at his feet.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish2016132_59-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish2016132-59">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>According to English, eventually, people who had forgotten or never learned the story began misinterpreting representations of it. That Saint Nicholas was shown with children led people to conclude he was the patron saint of children; meanwhile, the fact that he was shown with a barrel led people to conclude that he was the patron saint of brewers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish2016132–133_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish2016132–133-60">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to another story, during a great famine that Myra experienced in 311–312, a ship was in the port at anchor, loaded with wheat for the emperor in Constantinople. Nicholas invited the sailors to unload a part of the wheat to help in the time of need. The sailors at first disliked the request, because the wheat had to be weighed accurately and delivered to the emperor. Only when Nicholas promised them that they would not suffer any loss for their consideration did the sailors agree. When they arrived later in the capital, they made a surprising find: the weight of the load had not changed, although the wheat removed in Myra was enough for two full years and could even be used for sowing.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Relics">Relics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Relics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Gemile">Gemile</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Gemile">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Gemiler_Adas%C4%B1_kal%C4%B1nt%C4%B1lar%C4%B1.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Gemiler_Adas%C4%B1_kal%C4%B1nt%C4%B1lar%C4%B1.jpg/220px-Gemiler_Adas%C4%B1_kal%C4%B1nt%C4%B1lar%C4%B1.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Gemiler_Adas%C4%B1_kal%C4%B1nt%C4%B1lar%C4%B1.jpg/330px-Gemiler_Adas%C4%B1_kal%C4%B1nt%C4%B1lar%C4%B1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Gemiler_Adas%C4%B1_kal%C4%B1nt%C4%B1lar%C4%B1.jpg/440px-Gemiler_Adas%C4%B1_kal%C4%B1nt%C4%B1lar%C4%B1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="960" data-file-height="720" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Gemiler_Adas%C4%B1_kal%C4%B1nt%C4%B1lar%C4%B1.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Ruins of the fourth-century church on the island of <a href="/wiki/Gemiler_Island" title="Gemiler Island">Gemile</a>, where some scholars believe Saint Nicholas was originally entombed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKeys1993_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeys1993-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>It has long been traditionally assumed that Saint Nicholas was originally buried in his home town of Myra, where his relics are later known to have been kept,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint_44-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKeys1993_62-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeys1993-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> but some recent archaeological evidence indicates that Saint Nicholas may have originally been entombed in a rock-cut church located at the highest point on the small Turkish island of <a href="/wiki/Gemiler_Island" title="Gemiler Island">Gemile</a>, only twenty miles away from his birthplace of Patara. Nicholas's name is painted on part of the ruined building. In antiquity, the island was known as "Saint Nicholas Island"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKeys1993_62-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeys1993-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> and today it is known in Turkish as Gemiler Adasi, meaning "Island of Boats", in reference to Saint Nicholas's traditional role as the patron saint of seafarers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKeys1993_62-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeys1993-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The church was built in the fourth century, around the time of Nicholas's death,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKeys1993_62-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeys1993-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> and is typical of saints' shrines from that time period. Nicholas was the only major saint associated with that part of Turkey. The church where historians believe he was originally entombed is at the western end of the great processional way.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKeys1993_62-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeys1993-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Myra">Myra</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Myra">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Grab_Nikolaus.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Grab_Nikolaus.jpg/220px-Grab_Nikolaus.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Grab_Nikolaus.jpg/330px-Grab_Nikolaus.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Grab_Nikolaus.jpg/440px-Grab_Nikolaus.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="768" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Grab_Nikolaus.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Photograph of the desecrated sarcophagus in the <a href="/wiki/St._Nicholas_Church,_Demre" title="St. Nicholas Church, Demre">St. Nicholas Church, Demre</a>, where Saint Nicholas's bones were kept before they were removed and taken to Bari in 1087. <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJones1978176–193_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones1978176–193-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>In the mid-7th century, Gemile was vulnerable to attack by Arab fleets, so Nicholas's remains appear to have been moved from the island to the city of Myra, where Nicholas had served as bishop for most of his life. Myra is located roughly 40&#160;km (25&#160;mi) east of Gemile and its location further inland made it safer from seafaring Arab forces.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKeys1993_62-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeys1993-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>It is said that, in <a href="/wiki/Myra" title="Myra">Myra</a>, the relics of Saint Nicholas each year exuded a clear watery liquid which smelled like rose water, called <a href="/wiki/Manna" title="Manna">manna</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Myrrh" title="Myrrh">myrrh</a>, which was believed by the faithful to possess <a href="/wiki/Miracle" title="Miracle">miraculous</a> powers.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> As it was widely known that all Nicholas's relics were at Myra in their sealed sarcophagus, it was rare during this period for forgers of relics to claim to possess those belonging to Saint Nicholas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005135–136_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005135–136-65">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A solemn bronze statue of the saint by Russian sculptor Gregory Pototsky was donated by the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Russia" title="Government of Russia">Russian government</a> in 2000, and was given a prominent place in the square fronting the medieval Church of St Nicholas. In 2005, mayor Süleyman Topçu had the statue replaced by a red-suited plastic Santa Claus statue, because he wanted an image more recognisable to foreign visitors. Protests from the Russian government against this were successful, and the bronze statue was returned (albeit without its original high pedestal) to a corner nearer the church.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 28 December 2009, the Turkish government announced that it would be formally requesting the return of Saint Nicholas's skeletal remains to Turkey from the Italian government.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Turkish authorities asserted that Nicholas himself desired to be buried at his episcopal town, and that his remains were illegally removed from his homeland. In 2017, an archaeological survey at <a href="/wiki/St._Nicholas_Church,_Demre" title="St. Nicholas Church, Demre">St. Nicholas Church, Demre</a> was reported to have found a temple below the modern church, with excavation work to be done that will allow researchers to determine whether it still holds Nicholas's body.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Bari">Bari</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Bari">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:St._Nicholas_Bari.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/d/d5/St._Nicholas_Bari.jpg/220px-St._Nicholas_Bari.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/d/d5/St._Nicholas_Bari.jpg/330px-St._Nicholas_Bari.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/d/d5/St._Nicholas_Bari.jpg/440px-St._Nicholas_Bari.jpg 2x" data-file-width="480" data-file-height="360" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:St._Nicholas_Bari.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Basilica_di_San_Nicola" title="Basilica di San Nicola">Basilica di San Nicola</a> in <a href="/wiki/Bari" title="Bari">Bari</a>, Italy where most of the <a href="/wiki/Relics" class="mw-redirect" title="Relics">relics</a> of Saint Nicholas are kept today <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECullen2017_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECullen2017-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>After the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Manzikert" title="Battle of Manzikert">Battle of Manzikert</a> in 1071, the Byzantine Empire temporarily lost control over most of <a href="/wiki/Asia_Minor" class="mw-redirect" title="Asia Minor">Asia Minor</a> to the invading <a href="/wiki/Seljuk_Turks" class="mw-redirect" title="Seljuk Turks">Seljuk Turks</a>, and so <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Greeks" title="Byzantine Greeks">Greek Christians</a> of Myra became subjects of the Turks.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJones1978176–193_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones1978176–193-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005101_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005101-71">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> At the same time the Catholic Church in the West had declared (in 1054 AD) that the <a href="/wiki/Greek_Orthodox" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek Orthodox">Greek church</a>, the official church of the Byzantine Empire, was in <a href="/wiki/East-West_Schism" class="mw-redirect" title="East-West Schism">schism</a>. Because of the many wars in the region, some Christians were concerned that access to the tomb might become difficult.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJones1978176–193_63-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones1978176–193-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Taking advantage of the confusion and the loss by the Greek Christian community of Myra of its Byzantine imperial protection, in the spring of 1087, Italian sailors from <a href="/wiki/Bari" title="Bari">Bari</a> in <a href="/wiki/Apulia" title="Apulia">Apulia</a> seized part of the remains of the saint from his <a href="/wiki/St._Nicholas_Church,_Demre" title="St. Nicholas Church, Demre">burial church in Myra</a>, over the objections of the Greek Orthodox <a href="/wiki/Monasticism" title="Monasticism">monks</a> in the church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJones1978176–193_63-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones1978176–193-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Processione_di_San_Nicola_a_Bari.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Processione_di_San_Nicola_a_Bari.jpg/220px-Processione_di_San_Nicola_a_Bari.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="154" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Processione_di_San_Nicola_a_Bari.jpg/330px-Processione_di_San_Nicola_a_Bari.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Processione_di_San_Nicola_a_Bari.jpg/440px-Processione_di_San_Nicola_a_Bari.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4056" data-file-height="2832" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Processione_di_San_Nicola_a_Bari.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Procession of St Nicholas, <a href="/wiki/Bari" title="Bari">Bari</a></div></div></div>
<p>Adam C. English describes the removal of the relics from Myra as "essentially a holy robbery" and notes the thieves were not only afraid of being caught or chased after by the locals, but also the power of Saint Nicholas himself.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMedrano2017_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMedrano2017-75">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Returning to Bari, they brought the remains with them and cared for them. The remains arrived on 9 May 1087.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJones1978176–193_63-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones1978176–193-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint_44-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Two years later, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Urban_II" title="Pope Urban II">Pope Urban II</a> inaugurated a new church, the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_di_San_Nicola" title="Basilica di San Nicola">Basilica di San Nicola</a>, to Saint Nicholas in Bari. The Pope himself personally placed Nicholas's relics into the tomb beneath the altar of the new church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint_44-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The removal of Saint Nicholas's relics from Myra and their arrival in Bari is reliably recorded by multiple chroniclers, including <a href="/wiki/Orderic_Vitalis" title="Orderic Vitalis">Orderic Vitalis</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005131_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005131-76">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint_44-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> and 9 May continued to be celebrated every year by western Christians as the day of Nicholas's "translation".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint_44-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Eastern Orthodox Christians and the Turks have both long regarded the unauthorized removal of the relics from Myra as a blatant theft,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJones1978176–193_63-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones1978176–193-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal200593–94_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal200593–94-77">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> but the people of Bari have instead maintained that it was a rescue mission to save the bones from the Turkish invaders.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJones1978176–193_63-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones1978176–193-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005100–102_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005100–102-78">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> A legend, shown on the ceiling of the Basilica di San Nicola, holds that Nicholas once visited Bari and predicted that his bones would one day rest there.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal200593–94_77-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal200593–94-77">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Prior to the translation of Nicholas's relics to Bari, his cult had been known in western Europe, but it had not been extremely popular.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint_44-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In autumn of 1096, <a href="/wiki/Normans" title="Normans">Norman</a> and <a href="/wiki/Franks" title="Franks">Frankish</a> soldiers mustered in Bari in preparation for the <a href="/wiki/First_Crusade" title="First Crusade">First Crusade</a>. Although the Crusaders generally favored warrior saints, which Saint Nicholas was not, the presence of his relics in Bari made him materially accessible.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005114–115_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005114–115-79">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Nicholas's associations with aiding travelers and seafarers also made him a popular choice for veneration. Nicholas's veneration by Crusaders helped promote his cult throughout western Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005114–116_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005114–116-80">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the relics were brought to <a href="/wiki/Bari" title="Bari">Bari</a>, they continued to produce "myrrh", much to the joy of their new owners. Vials of myrrh from his relics have been taken all over the world for centuries, and can still be obtained from his church in Bari. Even up to the present day, a flask of manna is extracted from the tomb of Saint Nicholas every year on 6 December (the Saint's <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a>) by the clergy of the basilica. The <a href="/wiki/Myrrh" title="Myrrh">myrrh</a> is collected from a sarcophagus which is located in the basilica vault and could be obtained in the shop nearby. The liquid gradually seeps out of the tomb, but it is unclear whether it originates from the body within the tomb, or from the marble itself; since the town of Bari is a harbour, and the tomb is below <a href="/wiki/Sea_level" title="Sea level">sea level</a>, there have been several natural explanations proposed for the manna fluid, including the transfer of <a href="/wiki/Seawater" title="Seawater">seawater</a> to the tomb by <a href="/wiki/Capillary_action" title="Capillary action">capillary action</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In 1966, a vault in the crypt underneath the Basilica di San Nicola was dedicated as an Orthodox chapel with an <a href="/wiki/Iconostasis" title="Iconostasis">iconostasis</a> in commemoration of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic%E2%80%93Orthodox_Joint_Declaration_of_1965" title="Catholic–Orthodox Joint Declaration of 1965">recent lifting of the anathemas</a> the Roman Catholic and Eastern Orthodox Churches had issued against each other during the <a href="/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism" title="East–West Schism">Great Schism</a> in 1054.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005117_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005117-81">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>In May 2017, following talks between <a href="/wiki/Pope_Francis" title="Pope Francis">Pope Francis</a> and Russian Orthodox Patriarch Kirill, a portion of the relics of St. Nicholas in Bari were sent on loan to Moscow. The relic was on display for veneration at Christ the Savior Cathedral before being taken to Saint Petersburg in mid-June prior to returning to Bari.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> More than a million people lined up in Moscow for a momentary glimpse of the gilded ark holding one of the saint's ribs.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Venice">Venice</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Venice">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:San_Nicol%C3%B2_(Venice).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/San_Nicol%C3%B2_%28Venice%29.jpg/220px-San_Nicol%C3%B2_%28Venice%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="150" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/San_Nicol%C3%B2_%28Venice%29.jpg/330px-San_Nicol%C3%B2_%28Venice%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/San_Nicol%C3%B2_%28Venice%29.jpg/440px-San_Nicol%C3%B2_%28Venice%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6225" data-file-height="4243" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:San_Nicol%C3%B2_(Venice).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The church of <a href="/wiki/San_Nicol%C3%B2_al_Lido" class="mw-redirect" title="San Nicolò al Lido">San Nicolò al Lido</a> in Venice claims to hold roughly 500 bone fragments from Nicholas's skeleton,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017-84">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECullen2017_70-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECullen2017-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> which scientific examinations have confirmed are anatomically compatible with the bones in the Basilica di San Nicola in Bari.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017_84-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017-84">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMedrano2017_75-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMedrano2017-75">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>The sailors from Bari took only the main bones of Nicholas's skeleton, leaving all the minor fragments in the grave.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005136_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005136-85">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> The city of Venice had interest in obtaining the remaining fragments of his skeleton. In 1044, they dedicated the <a href="/wiki/San_Nicol%C3%B2_al_Lido" class="mw-redirect" title="San Nicolò al Lido">San Nicolò al Lido</a> monastery basilica to him on the north end of the <a href="/wiki/Lido_di_Venezia" title="Lido di Venezia">Lido di Venezia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005125–127_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005125–127-86">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> According to a single chronicle written by an anonymous monk at this monastery, in 1100, a fleet of Venetian ships accompanied by Bishop Henri sailed past Myra on their way to <a href="/wiki/Palestine_(region)" title="Palestine (region)">Palestine</a> for the <a href="/wiki/First_Crusade" title="First Crusade">First Crusade</a>. Bishop Henri insisted for the fleet to turn back and set anchor in Myra.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005127_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005127-87">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> The Venetians took the remaining bones of Saint Nicholas, as well as those of several other bishops of Myra, from the church there, which was only guarded by four Orthodox monks, and brought them to <a href="/wiki/Venice" title="Venice">Venice</a>, where they deposited them in the San Nicolò al Lido.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005127–136_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005127–136-88">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> This tradition was lent credence in two scientific investigations of the relics in Bari and Venice, which confirmed that the relics in the two cities are anatomically compatible and may belong to the same person.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017_84-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017-84">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> It is said that someone dies every time the bones of Saint Nicholas in Venice are disturbed. The last time the bones were examined was in July 1992.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005131_76-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005131-76">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_locations">Other locations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Other locations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Tomb_of_Saint_Nicholas" title="Tomb of Saint Nicholas">Tomb of Saint Nicholas</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Nicholas%27_Tomb.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/St_Nicholas%27_Tomb.jpg/220px-St_Nicholas%27_Tomb.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="330" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/St_Nicholas%27_Tomb.jpg/330px-St_Nicholas%27_Tomb.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d3/St_Nicholas%27_Tomb.jpg 2x" data-file-width="400" data-file-height="600" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Nicholas%27_Tomb.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Tomb_of_Saint_Nicholas" title="Tomb of Saint Nicholas">Tomb of Saint Nicholas</a> near Thomastown, Ireland</div></div></div>
<p>Because of Nicholas's skeleton's long confinement in Myra, after it was brought to Bari, the demand for pieces of it rose. Small bones quickly began to disperse across western Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005135–137_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005135–137-91">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> The sailors who had transported the bones gave one tooth and two fragments chipped from Nicholas's sarcophagus to the Norman knight <a href="/wiki/William_Pantulf" title="William Pantulf">William Pantulf</a>.
