Pantulf took these relics to his hometown of Noron in Normandy, where they were placed in the local Church of St. Peter in June 1092.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005136_85-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005136-85">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> In 1096, the duke of <a href="/wiki/Apulia" title="Apulia">Apulia</a> gave several bones of Saint Nicholas to the count of <a href="/wiki/Flanders" title="Flanders">Flanders</a>, which he then enshrined in the Abbey of <a href="/wiki/Watten,_Nord" title="Watten, Nord">Watten</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005136_85-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005136-85">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> According to legend, in 1101, Saint Nicholas appeared in a vision to a French clerk visiting the shrine at Bari and told him to take one of his bones with him to his hometown of <a href="/wiki/Saint-Nicolas-de-Port" title="Saint-Nicolas-de-Port">Port</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Nancy,_France" title="Nancy, France">Nancy</a>. The clerk took a finger bone back with him to Port, where a chapel was built to Saint Nicholas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005137_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005137-92">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Port became an important center of devotion in the cult of Nicholas and, in the fifteenth century, a church known as the <a href="/wiki/Saint-Nicolas,_Saint-Nicolas-de-Port" title="Saint-Nicolas, Saint-Nicolas-de-Port">Basilique Saint-Nicolas</a> was built there dedicated to him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005137_92-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005137-92">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> The town itself is now known as "Saint Nicolas de Port" in honor of Nicholas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint_44-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Medieval_Saint-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The clergy at Bari strategically gave away samples of Nicholas's bones to promote the cult and enhance its prestige. Many of these bones were initially kept in <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005137_92-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005137-92">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> but, after the <a href="/wiki/Sack_of_Constantinople_(1204)" class="mw-redirect" title="Sack of Constantinople (1204)">Sack of Constantinople</a> in 1204 during the <a href="/wiki/Fourth_Crusade" title="Fourth Crusade">Fourth Crusade</a>, these fragments were scattered across western Europe. A hand claimed to belong to Saint Nicholas was kept in the <a href="/wiki/San_Nicola_in_Carcere" title="San Nicola in Carcere">San Nicola in Carcere</a> in Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005137_92-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005137-92">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> This church, whose name means "Saint Nicholas in Chains", was built on the site of a former municipal prison. Stories quickly developed about Nicholas himself having been held in that prison.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish2016133_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish2016133-93">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> Mothers would come to the church to pray to Saint Nicholas for their jailed sons to be released and repentant criminals would place <a href="/wiki/Votive_offerings" class="mw-redirect" title="Votive offerings">votive offerings</a> in the church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish2016133_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish2016133-93">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> As a result of this, Nicholas became the patron saint of prisoners and those falsely accused of crimes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish2016133_93-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish2016133-93">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> An index finger claimed to belong to Saint Nicholas was kept in a chapel along the Ostian Way in Rome. Another finger was held in <a href="/wiki/Ventimiglia" title="Ventimiglia">Ventimiglia</a> in <a href="/wiki/Liguria" title="Liguria">Liguria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005137_92-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005137-92">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Today, many churches in Europe, Russia, and the United States claim to possess small relics, such as a tooth or a finger bone.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECullen2017_70-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECullen2017-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>An Irish tradition states that the relics of Saint Nicholas are also reputed to have been stolen from <a href="/wiki/Myra" title="Myra">Myra</a> by local Norman crusading knights in the twelfth century and buried near <a href="/wiki/Thomastown" title="Thomastown">Thomastown</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Kilkenny" title="County Kilkenny">County Kilkenny</a>, where a stone slab marks the site locally believed to be his grave.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> According to the Irish antiquarian <a href="/wiki/John_Hunt_(antiquarian)" title="John Hunt (antiquarian)">John Hunt</a>, the tomb probably actually belongs to a local priest from <a href="/wiki/Jerpoint_Abbey" title="Jerpoint Abbey">Jerpoint Abbey</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHunt1974_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHunt1974-96">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Scientific_analysis">Scientific analysis</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Scientific analysis">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Nicholas.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/76/Saint_Nicholas.jpg/170px-Saint_Nicholas.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="439" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/76/Saint_Nicholas.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="181" data-file-height="467" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Nicholas.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Saint Nicholas, <a href="/wiki/Russian_icon" class="mw-redirect" title="Russian icon">Russian icon</a> from first quarter of the 18th century (<a href="/wiki/Kizhi" class="mw-redirect" title="Kizhi">Kizhi</a> <a href="/wiki/Monastery" title="Monastery">monastery</a>, <a href="/wiki/Karelia" title="Karelia">Karelia</a>)</div></div></div>
<p>Whereas the devotional importance of <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relics</a> and the economics associated with pilgrimages caused the remains of most saints to be divided up and spread over numerous churches in several countries, Saint Nicholas is unusual in that most of his bones have been preserved in one spot: his grave crypt in <a href="/wiki/Bari" title="Bari">Bari</a>. Even with the allegedly continuing miracle of the manna, the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Bari-Bitonto" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Bari-Bitonto">archdiocese of Bari</a> has allowed for one scientific survey of the bones.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> In the late 1950s, while the <a href="/wiki/Crypt" title="Crypt">crypt</a> was undergoing much-needed restoration, the bones were removed from it for the first time since their interment in 1089. A special Pontiffical Commission permitted Luigi Martino, a professor of human anatomy at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Bari" title="University of Bari">University of Bari</a>, to examine the bones under the commission's supervision.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilkinson2018163_43-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilkinson2018163-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Martino took thousands of measurements, detailed scientific drawings, photographs, and x-rays.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilkinson2018163_43-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilkinson2018163-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> These examinations revealed the saint to have died at over seventy years of age and to have been of average height and slender-to-average build. He also suffered from severe chronic <a href="/wiki/Arthritis" title="Arthritis">arthritis</a> in his spine and pelvis.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilkinson2018163_43-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilkinson2018163-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2004, at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Manchester" title="University of Manchester">University of Manchester</a>, researchers Caroline Wilkinson and Fraco Introna reconstructed the saint's face based on Martino's examination.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilkinson2018163_43-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilkinson2018163-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> The review of the data revealed that the historical Saint Nicholas was 5&#160;ft 6&#160;in (1.68&#160;m) in height and had a broken nose, which had partially healed, revealing that the injury had been suffered <i>ante mortem</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-face_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-face-98">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-face2_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-face2-99">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> The broken nose appeared to conform with hagiographical reports that Saint Nicholas had been beaten and tortured during the <a href="/wiki/Diocletianic_Persecution" title="Diocletianic Persecution">Diocletianic Persecution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilkinson2018163_43-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilkinson2018163-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> The facial reconstruction was produced by Dr Caroline Wilkinson at the University of Manchester and was shown on a BBC2 TV program <i>The Real Face of Santa</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-face_98-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-face-98">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-face2_99-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-face2-99">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> In 2014, the Face Lab at <a href="/wiki/Liverpool_John_Moores_University" title="Liverpool John Moores University">Liverpool John Moores University</a> produced an updated reconstruction of Saint Nicholas's face.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilkinson2018163_43-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilkinson2018163-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2017, two researchers from <a href="/wiki/Oxford_University" class="mw-redirect" title="Oxford University">Oxford University</a>, Professor Tom Higham and Doctor Georges Kazan, <a href="/wiki/Radiocarbon_dating" title="Radiocarbon dating">radiocarbon dated</a> a fragment of a pelvis claimed to belong to Saint Nicholas. The fragment originally came from a church in <a href="/wiki/Lyon,_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Lyon, France">Lyon, France</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017_84-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017-84">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECoughlan2017_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoughlan2017-100">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECullen2017_70-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECullen2017-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> and, at the time of testing, was in the possession of Father Dennis O'Neill, a priest from St Martha of Bethany Church in Illinois.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017_84-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017-84">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECoughlan2017_100-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoughlan2017-100">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECullen2017_70-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECullen2017-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> The results of the radiocarbon dating confirmed that the pelvis dates to the fourth century AD, around the same time that Saint Nicholas would have died, and is not a medieval forgery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017_84-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017-84">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECoughlan2017_100-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoughlan2017-100">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECullen2017_70-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECullen2017-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> The bone was one of the oldest the Oxford team had ever examined.
</p><p>According to Professor Higham, most of the relics the team has examined turn out to be too recent to have actually belonged to the saint to whom they are attributed, but he states, "This bone fragment, in contrast, suggests that we could possibly be looking at remains from St Nicholas himself."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017_84-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017-84">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Dr Kazan believes the pelvis fragment may come from the same individual as the skeleton divided between the churches in Bari and Venice,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017_84-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017-84">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECoughlan2017_100-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoughlan2017-100">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECullen2017_70-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECullen2017-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> since the bone they tested comes from the left <a href="/wiki/Pubis_(bone)" title="Pubis (bone)">pubis</a>, and the only pelvis bone in the collection at Bari is the left <a href="/wiki/Ilium_(bone)" title="Ilium (bone)">ilium</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017_84-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Oxford2017-84">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> In the absence of DNA testing, however, it is not yet possible to know for certain whether the pelvis is from the same man.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECoughlan2017_100-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoughlan2017-100">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECullen2017_70-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECullen2017-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration_and_celebrations">Veneration and celebrations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Veneration and celebrations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Saint_Nicholas_Day" title="Saint Nicholas Day">Saint Nicholas Day</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87_-_Sveti_Nikola.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87_-_Sveti_Nikola.jpg/220px-Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87_-_Sveti_Nikola.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="292" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87_-_Sveti_Nikola.jpg/330px-Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87_-_Sveti_Nikola.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87_-_Sveti_Nikola.jpg/440px-Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87_-_Sveti_Nikola.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1765" data-file-height="2343" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87_-_Sveti_Nikola.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Saint Nicholas (<a href="/wiki/Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87" title="Uroš Predić">Uroš Predić</a>, 1903)</div></div></div>
<p>Among Greeks and Italians he is a favorite of sailors, <a href="/wiki/Fishermen" class="mw-redirect" title="Fishermen">fishermen</a>, ships and sailing. As a result, and over time, he has become the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of several cities which maintain <a href="/wiki/Harbour" class="mw-redirect" title="Harbour">harbours</a>. In centuries of Greek <a href="/wiki/Folklore" title="Folklore">folklore</a>, Nicholas was seen as "The Lord of the Sea", often described by modern Greek scholars as a kind of Christianized version of <a href="/wiki/Poseidon" title="Poseidon">Poseidon</a>. In modern Greece, he is still easily among the most recognizable saints and 6 December finds many cities celebrating their patron saint. He is also the patron saint of all of Greece and particularly of the <a href="/wiki/Hellenic_Navy" title="Hellenic Navy">Hellenic Navy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Pyh%C3%A4n_Nikolaoksen_katedraali_2.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Pyh%C3%A4n_Nikolaoksen_katedraali_2.jpg/220px-Pyh%C3%A4n_Nikolaoksen_katedraali_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="166" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Pyh%C3%A4n_Nikolaoksen_katedraali_2.jpg/330px-Pyh%C3%A4n_Nikolaoksen_katedraali_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Pyh%C3%A4n_Nikolaoksen_katedraali_2.jpg/440px-Pyh%C3%A4n_Nikolaoksen_katedraali_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4792" data-file-height="3624" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Pyh%C3%A4n_Nikolaoksen_katedraali_2.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Eastern Orthodox <a href="/wiki/Saint_Nicholas_Cathedral,_Kuopio" title="Saint Nicholas Cathedral, Kuopio">Saint Nicholas Cathedral</a> in <a href="/wiki/Kuopio,_Finland" class="mw-redirect" title="Kuopio, Finland">Kuopio, Finland</a></div></div></div>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Orthodox">Eastern Orthodox</a> Church, Saint Nicholas's memory is celebrated on almost every Thursday of the year (together with the <a href="/wiki/Twelve_apostles" class="mw-redirect" title="Twelve apostles">Apostles</a>) with special hymns to him which are found in the liturgical book known as the <a href="/wiki/Octoechos_(liturgy)" title="Octoechos (liturgy)">Octoechos</a>. Soon after the transfer of Saint Nicholas's relics from Myra to Bari, a Russian version of his <a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">Life</a> and an account of the transfer of his relics were written by a contemporary to this event.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Devotional <a href="/wiki/Akathist" title="Akathist">akathists</a> and <a href="/wiki/Canon_(hymnography)" title="Canon (hymnography)">canons</a> have been composed in his honour, and are frequently chanted by the faithful as they ask for his <a href="/wiki/Intercessory_prayer" class="mw-redirect" title="Intercessory prayer">intercession</a>. He is mentioned in the <a href="/wiki/Liturgy_of_Preparation" title="Liturgy of Preparation">Liturgy of Preparation</a> during the <a href="/wiki/Divine_Liturgy" title="Divine Liturgy">Divine Liturgy</a> (Eastern Orthodox <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">Eucharist</a>) and during the <a href="/wiki/All-Night_Vigil" class="mw-redirect" title="All-Night Vigil">All-Night Vigil</a>. Many Orthodox churches will have his <a href="/wiki/Icon" title="Icon">icon</a>, even if they are not named after him. In <a href="/wiki/Oriental_Orthodoxy" class="mw-redirect" title="Oriental Orthodoxy">Oriental Orthodoxy</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria">Coptic Church</a> observes the Departure of St. Nicholas on 10 Kiahk, or 10 Taḫśaś in Ethiopia, which corresponds to the Julian Calendar's 6 December and Gregorian Calendar's 19 December.<sup id="cite_ref-synaxarium_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-synaxarium-103">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-kiahk10_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kiahk10-104">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:De_Grey_Hours_f.57.r_St._Nicholas.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/De_Grey_Hours_f.57.r_St._Nicholas.png/220px-De_Grey_Hours_f.57.r_St._Nicholas.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="168" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/De_Grey_Hours_f.57.r_St._Nicholas.png/330px-De_Grey_Hours_f.57.r_St._Nicholas.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/De_Grey_Hours_f.57.r_St._Nicholas.png/440px-De_Grey_Hours_f.57.r_St._Nicholas.png 2x" data-file-width="1070" data-file-height="817" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:De_Grey_Hours_f.57.r_St._Nicholas.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Saint Nicholas depicted in a 14th-century English <a href="/wiki/Book_of_hours" title="Book of hours">book of hours</a></div></div></div>
<p>Nicholas had a reputation for secret gift-giving, such as putting coins in the shoes of those who left them out for him, a practice celebrated on his <a href="/wiki/Saint_Nicholas_Day" title="Saint Nicholas Day">feast day</a>, 6 December. For those who still observe the <a href="/wiki/Julian_calendar" title="Julian calendar">Julian calendar</a> the celebration currently takes place thirteen days later than it happens in the <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_calendar" title="Gregorian calendar">Gregorian calendar</a> and <a href="/wiki/Revised_Julian_calendar" title="Revised Julian calendar">Revised Julian calendar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Carus2002_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Carus2002-105">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Monaco" title="Monaco">Monaco</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_of_Our_Lady_Immaculate" title="Cathedral of Our Lady Immaculate">Cathedral of Our Lady Immaculate</a> was built from 1874 on the site of St Nicholas's church, founded in 1252. A children's Mass is still held on 6 December in the cathedral.
</p><p>In late <a href="/wiki/England_in_the_Middle_Ages" title="England in the Middle Ages">medieval England</a>, on Saint Nicholas Day parishes held <a href="/wiki/Christmastide" title="Christmastide">Christmastide</a> "<a href="/wiki/Boy_bishop" title="Boy bishop">boy bishop</a>" celebrations. As part of this celebration, youths performed the functions of priests and bishops, and exercised rule over their elders. Today, Saint Nicholas is still celebrated as a great gift-giver in several Western European and Central European countries. In medieval times, Christian <a href="/wiki/Nuns" class="mw-redirect" title="Nuns">nuns</a> in Belgium and France began to deposit baskets of food and clothes anonymously at the doorsteps of the needy, which gave rise to the practice of gift giving on Saint Nicholas Day.<sup id="cite_ref-DeeAnn2013_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DeeAnn2013-106">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> According to another source, on 6 December every sailor or ex-sailor of the <a href="/wiki/Low_Countries" title="Low Countries">Low Countries</a> (which at that time was virtually all of the male population) would descend to the harbour towns to participate in a church celebration for their patron saint. On the way back they would stop at one of the various <i>Nicholas fairs</i> to buy some hard-to-come-by goods, gifts for their loved ones and invariably some little presents for their children. While the real gifts would only be presented at Christmas, the little presents for the children were given right away, courtesy of Saint Nicholas. This and his miracle of him resurrecting the three butchered children made Saint Nicholas a patron saint of children and later students as well.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> The custom of giving gifts on Saint Nicholas Day is popular in various parts of Christendom, with a popular tradition including children placing their shoes in the <a href="/wiki/Foyer" class="mw-redirect" title="Foyer">foyer</a> for Saint Nicholas to deliver presents therein.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Santa_Claus" title="Santa Claus">Santa Claus</a> evolved from Dutch traditions regarding Saint Nicholas (<a href="/wiki/Sinterklaas" title="Sinterklaas">Sinterklaas</a>). When the Dutch established the colony of <a href="/wiki/New_Amsterdam" title="New Amsterdam">New Amsterdam</a>, they brought the legend and traditions of Sinterklaas with them.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Howard G. Hageman, of New Brunswick Theological Seminary, maintains that the tradition of celebrating Sinterklaas in New York existed in the early settlements of the <a href="/wiki/Hudson_Valley" title="Hudson Valley">Hudson Valley</a>, although by the early nineteenth century had fallen by the way.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/St._Nicholas_Park" title="St. Nicholas Park">St. Nicholas Park</a>, located at the intersection of St. Nicholas Avenue and 127th Street, in an area originally settled by Dutch farmers, is named for St. Nicholas of Myra.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nicholas is honored in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Lutheran)" title="Calendar of saints (Lutheran)">calendar of saints</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Lutheran_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran Church">Lutheran Churches</a>, as well as in <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">Church of England</a> and in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a> on 6 December.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Saint_Holy_Hierarch_Nicholas_of_Myra_in_Lycia.2C_Wonderworker_feast_days.5B111.5D"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Saint_Holy_Hierarch_Nicholas_of_Myra_in_Lycia,_Wonderworker_feast_days[111]">Saint Holy Hierarch Nicholas of Myra in Lycia, Wonderworker feast days<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Saint Holy Hierarch Nicholas of Myra in Lycia, Wonderworker feast days[111]">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>1 April – Beginning of the journey from Myra to Bari, in 1087</li>
<li>9 May – Translation of the relics of Saint Nicholas the Wonderworker from Myra to Bari, in 1087,</li>
<li>10 May – Passage of the <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relics</a> (sojourn) in 1087 of Saint Nicholas the Wonderworker through the island of <a href="/wiki/Zakynthos" title="Zakynthos">Zakynthos</a>, while on their way to <a href="/wiki/Bari" title="Bari">Bari</a>.</li>
<li>20 May – Arrival of the relics in Bari</li>
<li>29 July – Nativity of Saint Nicholas the Wonderworker</li>
<li>movable holiday on Sunday between 16 and 22 August – Synaxis of All Saints of Lefkada</li>
<li>22 September – Synaxis of All Saints of Tula (commemoration of the protection of Tula from the invasion of the Crimean Khan Devlet Giray in 1552)</li>
<li>6 December – commemoration of his death anniversary</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Iconography">Iconography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Iconography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nikolas_myra.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Nikolas_myra.jpg/220px-Nikolas_myra.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Nikolas_myra.jpg/330px-Nikolas_myra.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Nikolas_myra.jpg/440px-Nikolas_myra.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="480" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nikolas_myra.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox" class="mw-redirect" title="Russian Orthodox">Russian Orthodox</a> statue of Saint Nicolas, now in a corner near the church in <a href="/wiki/Demre" title="Demre">Demre</a></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ferapontov.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Ferapontov.jpg/220px-Ferapontov.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Ferapontov.jpg/330px-Ferapontov.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Ferapontov.jpg/440px-Ferapontov.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="400" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ferapontov.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Saint Nicholas, the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of Russian merchants. Fresco by <a href="/wiki/Dionisius" title="Dionisius">Dionisius</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Ferapontov_Monastery" title="Ferapontov Monastery">Ferapontov Monastery</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>Saint Nicholas is a popular subject portrayed on countless <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Orthodox">Eastern Orthodox</a> <a href="/wiki/Icons" class="mw-redirect" title="Icons">icons</a>, particularly <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church" title="Russian Orthodox Church">Russian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church" title="Serbian Orthodox Church">Serbian</a> ones. He is depicted as an Orthodox bishop, wearing the <a href="/wiki/Omophorion" title="Omophorion">omophorion</a> and holding a <a href="/wiki/Gospel_Book" title="Gospel Book">Gospel Book</a>. Sometimes he is depicted wearing the Eastern Orthodox <a href="/wiki/Mitre" title="Mitre">mitre</a>, sometimes he is bareheaded. Iconographically, Nicholas is depicted as an elderly man with a short, full, white, fluffy beard and balding head. In commemoration of the miracle attributed to him by tradition at the <a href="/wiki/First_Council_of_Nicaea" title="First Council of Nicaea">Council of Nicaea</a>, he is sometimes depicted with Christ over his left shoulder holding out a Gospel Book to him and the Theotokos over his right shoulder holding the omophorion. Because of his patronage of mariners, occasionally Saint Nicholas will be shown standing in a boat or rescuing drowning sailors; Medieval Chants and Polyphony, image on the cover of the Book of Hours of Duke of Berry, 1410.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nikola_from_1294.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Nikola_from_1294.jpg/150px-Nikola_from_1294.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="208" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Nikola_from_1294.jpg/225px-Nikola_from_1294.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Nikola_from_1294.jpg/300px-Nikola_from_1294.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1400" data-file-height="1941" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nikola_from_1294.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A large (184 cm in height) icon of St Nicholas painted in 1294 for the <a href="/wiki/Lipno_Church" title="Lipno Church">Lipno Church</a></div></div></div>
<p>In depictions of Saint Nicholas from Bari, he is usually shown as <a href="/wiki/Dark_skin" title="Dark skin">dark-skinned</a>, probably to emphasize his foreign origin.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005111_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005111-117">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> The emphasis on his foreignness may have been intended to enhance Bari's reputation by displaying that it had attracted the patronage of a saint from a far-off country.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal2005111_117-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal2005111-117">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> In Roman Catholic <a href="/wiki/Iconography" title="Iconography">iconography</a>, Saint Nicholas is depicted as a bishop, wearing the insignia of this dignity: a bishop's <a href="/wiki/Vestments" class="mw-redirect" title="Vestments">vestments</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Mitre" title="Mitre">mitre</a> and a <a href="/wiki/Crozier" class="mw-redirect" title="Crozier">crozier</a>. The episode with the three dowries is commemorated by showing him holding in his hand either three purses, three coins or three balls of gold. Depending on whether he is depicted as patron saint of children or sailors, his images will be completed by a background showing ships, children or three figures climbing out of a wooden barrel (the three slaughtered children he resurrected).<sup id="cite_ref-St._Nicholas_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-St._Nicholas-118">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In a strange twist, the three gold balls referring to the dowry affair are sometimes metaphorically interpreted as being oranges or other fruits. As in the <a href="/wiki/Low_Countries" title="Low Countries">Low Countries</a> in medieval times oranges most frequently came from Spain, this led to the belief that the Saint lives in Spain and comes to visit every winter bringing them oranges, other 'wintry' fruits and tales of magical creatures.<sup id="cite_ref-St._Nicholas_118-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-St._Nicholas-118">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Music">Music</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Music">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 1948, <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Britten" title="Benjamin Britten">Benjamin Britten</a> completed a <a href="/wiki/Cantata" title="Cantata">cantata</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Saint_Nicolas_(Britten)" title="Saint Nicolas (Britten)">Saint Nicolas</a></i> on a text by <a href="/wiki/Eric_Crozier" title="Eric Crozier">Eric Crozier</a> which covers the saint's legendary life in a dramatic sequence of events. A <a href="/wiki/Tenor" title="Tenor">tenor</a> soloist appears as Saint Nicolas, with a mixed choir, boys singers, strings, piano duet, organ and percussion.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: See also">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Nicholas&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-3"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-3">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span lang="el">Ἅγιος Νικόλαος</span>, <span title="Greek-language romanization"><i lang="el-Latn"><i>Hágios Nikólaos</i></i></span>; <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Sanctus Nicolaus</i></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-8"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-8">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The date of his birth and the year of his death are disputed,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal20052_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal20052-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> but 6 December has long been established as the traditional date of his death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal20052_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal20052-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Jeremy Seal remarks, "As vampires shun daylight, so saints are distinguished from ordinary mortals by the anniversaries they keep. The date of their death rather than their birth is commemorated."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeal20052–3_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeal20052–3-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-11"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-11">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><span title="Greek-language text"><span lang="el">Νικόλαος ὁ Θαυματουργός</span></span>, <span title="Greek-language romanization"><i lang="el-Latn"><i>Nikólaos ho Thaumaturgós</i></i></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-36"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-36">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Joe L. Wheeler and Jona Lendering both note that the legends of Saint Nicholas are filled with sets of three, which may be symbolic for Nicholas's vehement defense of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Trinity" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Trinity">Holy Trinity</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheeler201038_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler201038-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra_25-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELendering2006Nicholas_of_Myra-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>

<li id="cite_note-10"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-10">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFCunningham2005" class="citation book cs1">Cunningham, Lawrence (2005). <i>A brief history of saints</i>. Wiley-Blackwell. p.&#160;33. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-4051-1402-8" title="Special:BookSources/978-1-4051-1402-8"><bdi>978-1-4051-1402-8</bdi></a>. <q>The fourth-century Saint Nikolaos of Myra, Greek Anatolia (in present-day Turkey) spread to Europe through the port city of Bari in southern Italy... Devotion to the saint in the Low countries became blended with Nordic folktales, transforming this early <a href="/wiki/Greek_Orthodox" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek Orthodox">Greek Orthodox</a> Bishop into that Christmas icon, Santa Claus.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=A+brief+history+of+saints&amp;rft.pages=33&amp;rft.pub=Wiley-Blackwell&amp;rft.date=2005&amp;rft.isbn=978-1-4051-1402-8&amp;rft.aulast=Cunningham&amp;rft.aufirst=Lawrence&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ASaint+Nicholas" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Collins,_Ace_2009_121-12"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-Collins,_Ace_2009_121_12-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Collins,_Ace_2009_121_12-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Collins,_Ace_2009_121_12-2"><sup><i><b>c</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Collins,_Ace_2009_121_12-3"><sup><i><b>d</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFCollins,_Ace2009" class="citation book cs1">Collins, Ace (2009). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=HVPYqrdGhGEC&amp;q=Nicholas+born+Greek+city+of+Patara+270+AD"><i>Stories Behind Men of Faith</i></a>. Zondervan. p.&#160;121. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/9780310564560" title="Special:BookSources/9780310564560"><bdi>9780310564560</bdi></a>. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20150911140845/https://books.google.com/books?id=HVPYqrdGhGEC&amp;q=Nicholas+born+Greek+city+of+Patara+270+AD#v=snippet&amp;q=Nicholas%20born%20Greek%20city%20of%20Patara%20270%20AD&amp;f=false">Archived</a> from the original on 11 September 2015<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">28 November</span> 2015</span>. <q>Nicholas was born in the Greek city of Patara around 270 AD. The son of a businessman named Theophanes and his wife, Nonna, the child's earliest years were spent in Myra… As a port on the Mediterranean Sea, in the middle of the sea lanes that linked Egypt, Greece and Rome, Myra was a destination for traders, fishermen, and merchant sailors. Spawned by the spirit of both the city's Greek heritage and the ruling Roman government, cultural endeavours such as art, drama, and music were mainstays of everyday life.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Stories+Behind+Men+of+Faith&amp;rft.pages=121&amp;rft.pub=Zondervan&amp;rft.date=2009&amp;rft.isbn=9780310564560&amp;rft.au=Collins%2C+Ace&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DHVPYqrdGhGEC%26q%3DNicholas%2Bborn%2BGreek%2Bcity%2Bof%2BPatara%2B270%2BAD&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ASaint+Nicholas" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-Ingram,_W._Scott;_Ingram,_Asher_2004_24-29"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Ingram,_W._Scott;_Ingram,_Asher_2004_24_29-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFIngram,_W._ScottIngram,_Asher,_ScottRobert2004" class="citation book cs1">Ingram, W. He was very religious from an early age and devoted his life to Christianity.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Greek+Immigrants&amp;rft.pages=24&amp;rft.pub=Infobase+Publishing&amp;rft.date=2004&amp;rft.isbn=9780816056897&amp;rft.au=Ingram%2C+W.+Scott&amp;rft.au=Ingram%2C+Asher%2C+Scott&amp;rft.au=Robert&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ASaint+Nicholas" class="Z3988"></span><span class="cs1-maint citation-comment"><code class="cs1-code">{{<a href="/wiki/Template:Cite_book" title="Template:Cite book">cite book</a>}}</code>:  CS1 maint: multiple names: authors list (<a href="/wiki/Category:CS1_maint:_multiple_names:_authors_list" title="Category:CS1 maint: multiple names: authors list">link</a>)</span></span>

<li id="cite_note-Carus2002-105"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Carus2002_105-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFCarus2002" class="citation book cs1">Carus, Louise (1 October 2002). <i>The Real St. Nicholas</i>. Quest Books. p.&#160;2. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/9780835608138" title="Special:BookSources/9780835608138"><bdi>9780835608138</bdi></a>. <q>In Myra, the traditional St. Nicholas Feast Day is still celebrated on 6 December which many believe to be the anniversary of St Nicholas's death. This day is honored throughout Western Christendom, in lands comprising both Catholic and Protestant communities (in the Eastern Orthodox Church, the Saint's feast date is 19 December). On 5 December, the eve of St Nicholas Day, some American boys and girls put their shoes outside their bedroom door and leave a small gift in hopes that Saint Nicholas soon will be there.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Real+St.+Nicholas&amp;rft.pages=2&amp;rft.pub=Quest+Books&amp;rft.date=2002-10-01&amp;rft.isbn=9780835608138&amp;rft.aulast=Carus&amp;rft.aufirst=Louise&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ASaint+Nicholas" class="Z3988"></span></span>
